# Up-selling your ITM students Into Lessons
If you want your compelling offer to succeed, you have to nail the up-sell into regular lessons (or another programme). In this lesson we'll cover a few things we learned that help increase the conversion rate into on-going lessons.
*This lesson includes:*
Things to do in the final lesson to increase student conversion into regular lessons
Creating your course certificates
How to make the up-sell pitch
Tips on the post lesson follow up

*Making your course certificate:*
If your programme is for kids, then you definitely want to have a certificate to give out at the last lesson. Make sure you've got plenty pre-printed on site so they actually get the certificate in the last lesson.
Write their name and chosen instrument (if it's an ITM programme),
Take a photo individually with each student and their certificate and then a group photo *(see examples below)*

![image881.jpg](2. Business Backbones_images/image881.jpg)
![image610.jpg](2. Business Backbones_images/image610.jpg)
![image608.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image608.png)

A BIG LESSON we learned was that the admin team didn't know which photo was which student. So we trained the team to write the students name on the photo (see below) and save all the students photos for each class into a folder which was labeled by the class name and time (see pics below).
Another option is to name the photo file with the students name. It just depends which is easier for the device you're using to capture and send the photos.
![image606.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image606.png)
![image604.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image604.png)
![image618.jpg](2. Business Backbones_images/image618.jpg)
You can whip up certificates in  very quickly. There are stacks of pre-made templates that you can customise with your branding quickly.
Just adjust the colours to match your brand, and add your logo in.
If you click '*Templates*' then search the word '*Certificate*' you will see hundreds of templates *(see below)*
![image614.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image614.png)

*Making the pitch:*
Have each student share a favourite moment or favourite thing they've learned in your course.
If it's an 'intro to music' programme - have each student tell you their favourite instrument they'd like to continue on with. The teacher needs to make a note on their role or student management system about which instrument the student chose and if they mentioned they were keen to continue or not.
Write their name on the certificate. If it's an ITM programme, then include a line on the certificate saying 'I've chosen to learn the ____________' - then fill in the instrument.
One thing I would do differently now would be to give every student a 'What's next flyer' to take home with their certificate. In that I would congratulate them, explain that an email will be sent with their photos/videos, instructions about continuing with lessons, plus a very cool prize they can claim in - but only in the next [insert time period e.g 48hrs].
Tell students the photos will be emailed along with instructions on how they can get their prize [e.g. a movie ticket]
Show them some photos or a promo video about all the other cool opportunities they can experience if they carry on into regular lessons with you. We would highlight things like:
Some footage of some impressive students their age playing something cool.
Our student concerts
Our band programmes
Holiday programmes / camps
Our gamification system and prize cabinet - if you've got a rockstar roadmap you should give these students their first reward e.g badge, wristband, pin, hat, t-shirt etc
Propose they stick together as a class in this same time spot. Then they can continue learning together.

*Post lesson follow up:*
Consider keeping all the ITM students together in their regular time-slot. Open it as a new ongoing lesson time. This will help make the decision easier for parents as they’re already used to the schedule and they know their child will be alongside friends they’ve already made.
Send an email after the final class. Here's what the email needs to include:
Congratulate the student on completing the course and getting their certificate
Send the photos / video links from the class photo shoot etc - We used to send individual emails with each child's photos. This got far too time consuming, so we eventually just attached a link to the google folder with all the photos to cut the work down.
 '[Students name] said they would like to continue into regular [chosen instrument] classes', - then list the next steps to register into the best class / time slot etc.
Include an offer in the email with a deadline. E.g We actually have a special right now where if you register by [x-date], then [students name] will get a [insert cool prize kid would like + a discount the parent would like]
Ideas that kids care about:
Movie vouchers
Shopping mall vouchers
Amazon vouchers
McDonald's vouchers (or other fast food nearby kids love)
Swimming pool voucher
Voucher to other cool stores e.g trampoline park, go-karting, mini golf, ice cream store etc... (Whatever is cool and convenient in your town/city)
Ideas parents care about:
Free registration fee
First lesson free
$50 off first month
First month half price
Free welcome pack with workbook + other resources e.g drum sticks, guitar picks, Metronome
Follow up the email with a phone calls and texts until you get them registered to continue or a solid 'No'.
For any students that decide not to continue, keep them on your email list so they continue to receive promotions and newsletters etc.

### A = Assets……………….
# Securing Your Online Assets
This lesson is going to be looking at securing your online assets. There are a number of key things you need to set up and below we will go through the steps on how to do this for the following:
Purchasing your Domain
Setting up a custom domain name in gmail
Google my business
Business FaceBook and Instagram page
Business YouTube page

*Domain*
A domain is a human-readable and memorable label that serves as an address for resources on the internet. It is used to uniquely identify websites, email servers, and other online services. Domains are a crucial part of the internet's infrastructure, providing a user-friendly way to access websites and online content.
Domains are essential for several reasons:
Online Identity: A domain provides a distinct and personalised online identity. It's like a digital "address" for your online presence.
Professionalism: A custom domain lends credibility to your website or email, making you appear more professional and trustworthy to visitors or recipients.
Branding: A well-chosen domain can reinforce your brand and make it easier for people to remember your website or business name.
Control: Having your own domain gives you control over your online presence. You can switch hosting providers, create custom email addresses, and make changes to your website as needed.
Accessibility: Domains make it easy for users to find your website or services by typing a memorable name into their browsers.

What Domains Are Used For:
Websites: Domains are primarily used to access websites. When you type a domain name into your browser, it connects to the corresponding web server, which then delivers the website's content to your screen.
Email: Domains are also used for email addresses. Businesses and individuals can set up custom email addresses associated with their domain (e.g., contact@yourdomain.com).
Branding and Marketing: A domain can play a significant role in branding and marketing efforts, as it becomes an integral part of your online identity.
E-Commerce: Domains are crucial for online stores, enabling customers to find and shop on your website.
Information Sharing: Domains are used for blogs, forums, news sites, and more, allowing individuals and organisations to share information and engage with audiences.

How to Get a Domain:
Choose a Registrar: Select a domain registrar (e.g., GoDaddy, Namecheap, Google Domains) where you can register your domain. Look for a reputable registrar with reasonable pricing and good customer support.
Search for Availability: Use the registrar's domain search tool to see if your desired domain name is available. If it's taken, the tool might suggest alternative variations or extensions.
Choose Your Domain: Once you find an available domain you like, proceed to add it to your cart.
Provide Information: During the checkout process, you'll need to provide your contact information and select how long you want to register the domain (typically in yearly increments).
Payment: Complete the payment process to secure the domain. Prices can vary based on factors like the domain extension (.com, .net, .org, etc.).
DNS Settings: Access your domain's DNS settings in your registrar's control panel. Here, you can set up DNS records to point your domain to a web hosting provider or email service.
Website or Email Setup: Depending on your needs, you can set up a website by choosing a hosting provider and building your site. Alternatively, you can configure email services associated with your domain.
Domain Privacy (Optional): Consider enabling domain privacy protection to keep your personal information hidden from the public WHOIS database.

*Setting up a custom domain name in gmail…*
Here are some steps to setting up a custom domain name in gmail:
Purchase a Domain: see above.
Access Gmail Settings: Log in to your Gmail account. Click on the gear icon in the upper right corner and select "See all settings."
Accounts and Import: In the settings menu, click on the "Accounts and Import" tab.
Add Another Email Address: Under the "Send mail as" section, click on "Add another email address."
Enter Your Name and Email Address: A pop-up window will appear. Enter your name and the custom email address you want to set up (e.g., you@yourdomain.com).
Treat as an Alias: Decide whether you want to treat this address as an alias. If you check this option, outgoing messages will still appear to come from your Gmail address, but replies will go to the custom address.
SMTP Server Settings: Choose the "Send through Gmail" option and click "Next." In the next screen, enter the SMTP server settings for your custom domain's email provider. These settings are typically available from your email hosting provider. If you're using G Suite or Google Workspace, you can find these details in the admin console.
Verification Code: Gmail will send a verification code to the custom email address you provided. Check your custom email inbox and enter the verification code in the pop-up window.
Confirmation: Once the code is verified, you'll be prompted to confirm that you want to send emails from your custom email address. You can also set it as the default address for outgoing emails if desired.
Verification Process: Gmail will send a confirmation email to your custom email address. You need to click the confirmation link in that email to complete the setup.
Compose and Send: After the verification is successful, you can compose a new email in Gmail. In the "From" field, you'll see a drop-down arrow that lets you choose which email address you want to send from.
DNS Configuration (Optional): If you want to receive emails sent to your custom domain in Gmail, you'll need to configure MX (Mail Exchange) records in your domain's DNS settings. The exact MX records you need will depend on your email hosting provider.

*Google My Business*
In this video below Jonny shows you all the key elements of google my business and what elements you need to collect to optimise it.
Optimising your Google My Business (GMB) listing is crucial for improving your local search visibility and ranking higher on Google. Here's a list of instructions to help you maximise your GMB listing:
Claim or Verify Your Listing: If you haven't already, claim your business on Google My Business. If it's already listed, verify your ownership through a postcard or other verification methods.
Complete Your Profile: Fill out every field in your GMB profile, including business name, address, phone number (NAP), website URL, hours of operation, and category.
Choose the Right Category: Select the most relevant primary and secondary categories that accurately describe your business. Be specific; this helps Google understand your business better.
Add High-Quality Photos: Include clear, high-resolution photos of your business exterior, interior, products, services, and team. These images build trust and help potential customers understand your offerings.
Write a Compelling Business Description: Craft a concise and engaging business description that highlights your unique selling points and important keywords. Be informative and customer-focused.
Upload Posts Regularly: Use the GMB posts feature to share updates, promotions, events, and news about your business. Posts can enhance engagement and provide valuable information to users.
Collect and Respond to Reviews: Encourage customers to leave reviews on your GMB listing. Respond promptly and professionally to both positive and negative reviews to show your engagement and commitment to customer satisfaction.
Add Attributes and Amenities: Include relevant attributes such as wheelchair accessibility, outdoor seating, Wi-Fi availability, and more. These details help customers find exactly what they're looking for.
Set Up Messaging: Enable messaging to allow customers to contact you directly through the GMB listing. Respond promptly to inquiries to improve customer experience.
Publish Q&A: Address frequently asked questions in the Q&A section of your listing. This can help potential customers find essential information quickly.
Use Google My Business Insights: Regularly review the insights section to gain insights into how customers are interacting with your listing. This information can guide your optimisation efforts.
Geo-Tagged Images: Ensure that your images have geo-tags, which help reinforce your business's location and improve local search relevance.
Local Citations and Consistency: Ensure your business's name, address, and phone number are consistent across all online platforms, directories, and social media. This boosts your credibility.
Website Optimisation: Link your GMB listing to your website and ensure that your website is mobile-friendly, fast, and optimised for local search keywords.
Use Posts for Events and Offers: Utilise GMB posts to share time-sensitive information such as upcoming events, special offers, discounts, and sales.
Respond to Messages Promptly: If you enable messaging, respond quickly to customer inquiries. This responsiveness is valued by potential customers and can positively impact your ranking.
Optimise for Voice Search: Use natural language in your description and posts to align with how people use voice search on devices like Google Assistant.
Regular Updates: Keep your GMB listing up-to-date with any changes to your business hours, services, or contact information.

Remember that optimising your Google My Business listing is an ongoing process. Regularly monitor and adjust your strategy based on changes in your business and customer behaviour, and keep up with any updates or features Google adds to the platform.

*Business FaceBook and Instagram Page*
It’s pretty obvious now that you can greatly benefit from having a strong presence on social media platforms like Facebook and Instagram. These platforms provide a powerful way to connect with students, parents, and the community, ultimately boosting visibility, engagement, and enrolment. With the visual appeal of Instagram and the wide reach of Facebook, you can showcase your offerings, share success stories, and build a loyal following that can positively impact their growth.

Below are some brief steps on setting up your business pages.
Facebook:
Visit Facebook.com and sign in with your personal account.
Click the downward arrow in the top right corner and select "Create Page."
Choose the "Business or Brand" option and follow the prompts to enter your music school's information.
Add a profile picture and cover photo that represent your school visually.
Complete your "About" section with a concise description, contact details, and a link to your website.
Start posting engaging content related to music education, lessons, student performances, and school updates.

Instagram:
Download the Instagram app from your device's app store and sign up or log in.
Tap the profile icon on the bottom right, then tap the hamburger menu on the top right and select "Settings."
Scroll down and tap "Switch to Professional Account," then select "Music."
Choose the most relevant category, enter your contact info, and connect your Instagram account to your Facebook page (if you have one).
Customise your profile with a profile picture, bio, and link to your website.
Start sharing captivating photos and short videos that showcase your music school's environment, instructors, and students.

![image195.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image195.png)
Remember to engage regularly with your audience, respond to comments and messages, and use relevant hashtags to increase your content's discoverability.
*Business YouTube Page*
It’s important to remember that Youtube is also a search engine, this means it’s important to load an SEO related video in. Once you video is in you then need to make keyword search titles e.g. Music lessons [your town], guitar lessons [your town].
Setting Up a Business Page on YouTube:
Create a Google Account:
If you don't already have one, create a Google Account. This account will serve as the foundation for your YouTube channel.
Create Your YouTube Channel:
Sign in to your Google Account and visit YouTube.com.
Click on your profile picture (top right corner) and select "Your channel."
Click "Create channel" and choose the "Use a custom name" option to name your channel after your music school.
Channel Customisation:
Add a profile picture and cover image that reflect your music school's identity.
Write an engaging channel description that highlights what your school offers and what viewers can expect to find on your channel.
Upload Quality Content:
Create and upload videos that showcase your music school's activities, student performances, lessons, and more.
Ensure videos are of high quality in terms of both visuals and audio.
Video Optimisation:
Use descriptive titles, relevant keywords, and detailed descriptions for your videos to improve search visibility.
Add tags that are relevant to the content of each video.
Engagement and Interaction:
Encourage viewers to like, comment, and subscribe to your channel. Engaging with your audience fosters a sense of community.
Respond promptly to comments and messages to show that you value interaction.
Promote Your Channel:
Share your YouTube videos on your other social media platforms, website, and in your email communications.
Collaborate with local musicians, influencers, or other music-related channels to cross-promote content.
Consistency:
Establish a regular posting schedule to keep viewers engaged and informed about upcoming events and content.
Monitor Analytics:
Keep an eye on YouTube Analytics to understand your audience, track video performance, and refine your content strategy over time.
Create Playlists:
Organise your videos into playlists, making it easier for viewers to find content that interests them.
Use Calls to Action:
Include calls to action in your videos, such as encouraging viewers to visit your website, enrol in classes, or attend events.

Remember that YouTube is a platform where consistency and quality matter. Focus on creating content that resonates with your target audience and adds value to their musical journey. Over time, your YouTube channel can become a valuable resource for showcasing your music school's expertise and passion.
![image195.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image195.png)

When you're making any visual items, such as posters... Use Canva as the has all the right sizing you need for items such as FaceBook Banners etc.

# Introduction - Watch First
Welcome to this mini-course called "Winning Websites".
We've researched hundreds of music school owners' websites to figure out what is the best formula when it comes to creating a winning website, no matter how big or small your music school is.
This massive database filled with examples, advice and layout ideas is designed to make your life easier when it comes to creating the perfect website. We have given you all the tools, systems and inspiration you need to create a website that both looks professional but also brings in qualified leads and plenty of new students.

The content for this module is broken into 12 sections:
*1. Start here: Workbook and Important Tips:*
We've included a workbook for you to access and fill out as you work through the lesson content. We've also addressed some tips for doing a DIY website verse finding a designer. Even if you've already got a working website, it's worth checking out these options to make sure your website is designed in the easiest, most sustainable and cost-effective way possible.
* *
*2. Homepages:*
This is a massive lesson filled with website examples and options for what are the best features to include on your homepage. Whether you have a very large music school or a smaller one, this lesson will help you break down what's most important to go on the front page that users will often see first.

*3. Main Offer Pages:*
Sometimes a music school will offer so many programs, it can be hard to choose the best way to showcase this to potential families. This lesson will guide you through breaking down your offers into what's most important to showcase first, to new leads.

*4. About/Team Pages:*
Often left till last, the about page is an integral part of your website. It's the point of difference that helps your music school stand out, and connect to your website visitors in a special and unique way. This lesson will help you design an about page that gets people lining up to visit your music school specifically!

*5. Policy Pages:*
As a general rule, if you're collecting user information on a website like a person's name and contact details, your website will need a privacy policy. This doesn't need to be intimidating, however. This lesson will help you create a privacy policy quickly and in a visually appealing way.

*6. Blog/News Pages:*
A regularly updated blog page is great for both your SEO and the way your school stands out to potential customers. This lesson will address best practices for maintaining a current blog or news page, and how to lay it out in an easy-to-navigate way

*7. Facebook Page Content:*
How do you link your Facebook, YouTube, Instagram and other social accounts to your website? These platforms should be "talking" to each other regularly, to increase awareness and ultimately sales. Access this lesson to learn about how to make the most out of the connection between your website and social media content.

*8. Individual Instrument Pages:*
Whilst some people make the mistake of simply saying "we offer guitar lessons" and outlining all the boring details on these instrument pages, we're going to help you turn your instrument pages into sales machines. This lesson will help you create lead pages out of your instrument pages, so you can easily get more leads from your Facebook ads and other marketing channels.

*9. Other Offers:*
Where do you put the information about your instrument rental program, your charity events and the school holiday camps you run at Christmas time? This lesson will address how to showcase these extra offers to new families without cluttering your main website, so the website user doesn't feel overwhelmed.

*10. Unique Features:*
There are a few tools that can help improve the user experience on your website and help potential customers feel connected to you and your music school. We address some great ideas in this lesson, with examples to see from other websites

*11. Login Portals:*
Just a quick one here. This lesson addresses the best places to pop a button so your students can access their online portal. The benefit of putting this on your website is that it'll rank higher on Google as students keep accessing your website on a weekly basis!

*12. Conclusion:*
        Final thoughts and your opportunity to give any feedback or additional collaborations

# Workbook and Important Tips
Whether you are here for the first time without ever having built a website, or you have an existing website that you would like to update to be the best it can be, this training is for you.
Here are a few essential things to prepare you for the upcoming lessons.
*Contents:*
The Workbook ()
Should I DIY or Freelance?
The Importance of Landing Pages
This Module vs the SEO Module

*The Workbook:*
We want to make the process of creating your dream website as seamless as possible. There's no point just reading the lessons, you need to take action!
We've included a 20+ page workbook for you to access and fill out as you work through the lesson content.
Consider this workbook your professional design brief. As you work through the checklists, you'll be making important decisions as to the style, content and layout of your website. This design brief can then be used to DIY your website or passed on to a designer to create it for you.

### ACCESS YOUR WINNING WEBSITES WORKBOOK HERE

****Please note: Any google docs shared in the BAM library require you to be logged into a Gmail account in order to view them. We do not give editing access to the original file so you need to click 'File' then 'Make a copy'. This will generate an editable version of your own that you can save into your own drive folders or download. *

*Troubleshooting:*
*If you’re not able to 'make a copy', then you’re not viewing this document while being logged into a Gmail account. You need to log into your Gmail account, then try open the link and click ‘make a copy’.*
*This is a google sheet, so if you don’t have a Gmail account to access this, then you will need to set one up. it's free. *

Now, let's discuss what would suit your own needs best...

*Should I DIY my website or hire a freelancer? *
There are many factors to consider here. What is your budget? How much time do you have? What is your experience with website design? Even... how much patience do you have!?
Hiring a freelancer will cost you more money, but it can be the most time-efficient way to design your website, provided you know exactly what it is that you want (this is where the above design workbook will come in handy). However, if you're looking to put together something simple on a budget, there are great websites that let you put together something that looks great, with a drag-n-drop editor.
Before we share our favourite places to go for website design, here's an interesting bit of data to consider. We recently polled the #BAMSQUAD to see how much money members spent to have their websites built. Interestingly, an overwhelmingly large number of members chose to DIY their website or get volunteer assistance.
If this is the option you are considering too, be sure to make the most of the workbook that's in this course so that you can have the best possible plan of attack for when you get into the nuts and bolts of building the site online!
![image131.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image131.png)

*DIY Low-Cost Options (In order of our preference):*
The benefit of DIY options is you are fully in control of editing the website and you don't need to pay a designer every single time you make edits. It's also cheaper to do this than hire somebody. The downside is of course that you need spend valuable time designing your own site.
However, some website designers can build you a site on a DIY platform of your choice (such as a Squarespace designer) which could be a great "middle ground" in terms of it being more cost-effective and also allowing you the option to make updates yourself in the future.
### 1: Squarespace
Squarespace is the most friendly to Google in terms of SEO functionality. It's a slightly more expensive option than the other DIY options below, but this is because of the quality of your product. Squarespace websites always work perfectly on mobile and tablet devices which is absolutely essential if you want your website to get found online. They also have plenty of pre-made templates to choose from, that allows you to more quickly have a finished product.
The downside of Squarespace is not every single thing is customisable so if you have a dream website design in mind, Squarespace may not accommodate that specific layout.

### 2. Weebly
Weebly is sometimes seen as the forgotten cousin of Wix. But bigger marketing (Wix) doesn't always mean better. Weebly has excellent intuitive design options and templates to make your website building life easier. An advantage of Weebly is that the edits made are automatically updated on mobile/tablet devices and it's overall much more mobile friendly.
The quality and feel of a Weebly site is poorer then Squarespace but to general user, this isn't too noticeable. Keep in mind that mistakes in Weebly are costly - you can't go "back" to previous edits if you make a mistake. There are great SEO options in Weebly as well.

### 3. Wix
Wix is a great option if you have a specific website layout in mind and you'd like to make almost an exact replica yourself. They generally work out to be cheaper than Squarespace, however, you will be spending more time on edits, as every change you make then has to be updated in mobile and then tablet view as well.
Wix isn't as SEO and mobile-friendly as Squarespace but it's the most flexible option available for designing your site exactly how you want!
![image233.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image233.png)
4.
A lot of people are drawn to Wordpress initially because of how cheap it appears, to get started. However, some of the hidden costs include the need to purchase a "theme" to use alongside this platform and the fact that you'll need to purchase your own hosting every year. A lot of times, Wordpress users run into technical errors that mean they need to pay a support or technical person to fix issues on their website.
That being said, if you've got coding experience Wordpress could be a great option for you as it's the most friendly in terms of SEO (if set up correctly) and it has the most customizable options if you understand coding and website design. A lot of freelancers will use Wordpress when designing a site for you.

*Professional Freelance Options:*
If you have a decent marketing budget and you'd rather spend your time on other valuable tasks to move your business forward, you may consider hiring someone to design the website for you. If you choose this option, do research upfront, to gauge the approximate cost of the website design.

*Some Freelance hiring tips:*
Be sure to reach out to multiple designers and discuss your options with each of them. Use your design brief workbook, as a reference to ask for exactly what you want. Consider things that will make your designing process cheaper such as asking them to start with a pre-existing template or offering to them that you can load all the images and text in yourself. This will work only if the designer creates the website in a program that you can learn how to edit in the future.
Try to find a designer who can give you access to editing and making changes yourself, as your music school expands. Check out the portfolio of each designer and even look their sites up online, to test the loading speed of their finished work. When choosing a designer, jump on a few Zoom calls to see if you'd be a great fit as you'll be working together quite a bit in those early stages of the design process.  Ask lots of questions and don't be afraid to say no if a designer can't meet your expectations of budget, timeframe and what you need!
### 5. Upwork
Upwork is a great site for having people sell themselves to you, instead of having to do the groundwork of finding the best designer. If you post a really clear brief of what you want (which will be easy once your workbook is filled out!), then you can let the designer offers trickle in. Look at their budget, their portfolios and shortlist a few designers who can ask questions with and jump on a Zoom call with a couple to find the best fit for you.
### 6. Freelancer
Freelancer also has a lot of high quality designers on the platform. Here, designers "bid" on your project with some type of brief for what they could build for you. All you have to do is browse the designs and if you like it, choose a designer, pay them, and start working on the project!

### 7. Fiverr
This freelance site differs to Freelancer and Upwork, as you reach out to designers instead of asking them to bid on your job. Fiverr can be great if you want a simple website outline set up, which you can customise later. It can also be great for finding super affordable designers - as long as you're willing to sift through a lot of not suitable people to start with!

*8. Your Local FB Group Website Guru:*
See if there's a "small business" Facebook group in your local area, hop on here and see if you can request support for building your winning website. Plenty of people will jump at the opportunity to sell you their services and see if you'd like to bring them on board!

* The Importance of a "Landing Page" Focused Website:*
Just a quick note here.
We reference the concept of a "landing page" themed website very frequently in this Winning Websites course. A landing page (or lead page) is a standalone website that doesn't link back to your homepage or anywhere else on your site. It's the page that your leads "land" on when they click through on an ad or another form of marketing such as a backlink on a local school's website. The purpose of a landing page is that it is low distraction and it encourages your lead to take direct action to reach out and book a lesson with your music school.
A lot of times, we've found that music school owners don't have the time or resources to create separate landing pages for all their offers. So instead, we've made a point of teaching you the elements of a good landing page, so you can consistently include these in your website.
You should aim to have the important elements of a good landing page on all your main pages on your website. Including your about page, the home page, contact page, instrument pages etc. You will learn what these elements are, in the course. Just keep in mind the importance of these, to drive your leads directly to reaching out and booking in with your music school!

*SEO (Getting found on Google):*
This Winning Websites module is all about how to design a website that is user friendly, functional and attracts leads through your marking funnel so they end up signing up for lessons! '
However, what we don't cover here is how to GET found on Google. There's an entire training we did recently, titled "SEO for Music Schools" in Module 5 "Marketing a Movement". We highly recommend you check this out, so you can do your SEO right the first time and make small notes on your paper.

The most important thing to note about this website training is that the hundreds of studio examples that can be used for inspiration, is what is going to make this invaluable as you work on on designing your own website.
Take all the time you can to really dive into the examples and make decision for what you want, based on these websites for inspiration!

That's it. Let's dive in!

# Landing Pages
*What is a Landing Page*
A landing page is a dedicated web page designed to captivate and convert your website visitors into potential students. It's a virtual storefront that showcases the essence of your music school while guiding visitors towards your call to action – whether it's signing up for lessons, requesting more information, or even enrolling into lessons.
Your landing page may start as a sign up for any instrument or offer that you have, however as you grow you want to have different pages for different offers. Therefore when you get a lead for guitar, the link that the potential customer clicks on takes them straight to the guitar offer page with a really clear CTA... This makes it as easy as possible for the perspective customer to sign up.

*Why are Landing Pages Important*
Landing pages serve as a strategic tool to highlight the unique offerings of your music school in a concise and compelling manner. Here's why they're indispensable:
Focused Conversion: Landing pages are created with a specific goal in mind, making them exceptionally effective in converting visitors into leads or customers. For music schools, this translates to more inquiries, registrations, and enrolments.
Tailored Message: Craft a targeted message that resonates with your audience's musical aspirations and educational needs. Highlight your school's strengths, faculty expertise, diverse programs, and success stories to connect with potential students.
Enhanced User Experience: Landing pages are designed for simplicity and clarity. This streamlined experience guides visitors directly to the information they're seeking, minimising distractions and friction.

*The Key Elements of a High Impact Landing Page*
Compelling Headline: Grab attention with a clear, concise headline that addresses the main benefit or solution your music school offers.
Engaging Visuals: Use captivating images, videos, and graphics that showcase your school's environment, instruments, and talented instructors.
Unique Selling Proposition (USP): Clearly state what sets your music school apart – whether it's personalised instruction, innovative teaching methods, or a track record of successful students.
Call to Action (CTA): Place a prominent and action-oriented CTA button that guides visitors towards the desired action, such as "Schedule a Trial Lesson" or "Enrol Now."
Testimonials and Success Stories: Share real stories of satisfied students and their achievements to build trust and credibility.
Program Overviews: Provide concise descriptions of your music programs, classes, workshops, and other offerings.
Benefits and Features: Clearly outline the benefits students will gain from choosing your music school, including improved skills, performance opportunities, and a supportive community.
Contact Information: Make it easy for visitors to get in touch with you by displaying contact details or a contact form.
Social Proof: Showcase any awards, affiliations, or recognition your music school has received within the industry.
Responsive Design: Ensure your landing page is optimised for mobile devices, offering a seamless experience across all screen sizes.
By creating a captivating landing page, your music school can make a lasting impression, convey its unique value proposition, and drive prospective students to take action. Remember, a well-crafted landing page is not just a web page – it's a gateway to fostering a harmonious connection between your school and aspiring musicians.

# Home Pages
This is a massive lesson filled with website examples and options for what are the best features to include on your homepage. Whether you have a very large music school or a smaller one, this lesson will help you break down what's most important to go on the front page that users will often see first.
The checklist on this page matched with the workbook on how to create your own homepage will be very helpful as you decide on what content to include in your own site. The full-page examples are also going to be a game-changer in helping you decide exactly what layout you want on your website!

*Contents:*
Examples - List of homepages used in this post with preview images
Purpose - General overview of homepages
Music School Websites - General observation of music school website homepages
How to use this checklist
Checklist - An explanation of the list item followed by examples and comments
Full Examples - All of the homepages used here in full

1. Examples:
Here are the music school homepages used throughout this post. We highly recommend clicking on and scrolling through each of these before you move on. It will give you the context you need to get the most out of the content that comes afterwards.

2. Purpose:
Home pages are typically the first entry point to a website for most users. They also function as a fallback page that users return to so they can better navigate a site.
This means your homepage has to get people's attention and help them find key information quickly and easily. Key information doesn’t just mean what your business is and who it’s for, it's also important that the categories and subcategories of your business are clearly defined and laid out to create obvious pathways for different users.

3. Music School Websites:
The more your school grows the more difficult it becomes to make a website and a homepage that is compelling, easy to navigate, and clearly lays out key information. Larger schools accumulate more offers, more categories of offers, and more locations.
That being said, larger schools might find it easier to be compelling for the same reason, but this issue of not overwhelming visitors without missing key information is still going to be an ongoing challenge.

4. Using This Checklist:
Every business is different. Not everything on this list needs to be on your site. This is more of a way to guide and supplement what you're already working with. Whether you're building, rebuilding, or just updating parts of your website this checklist will hopefully be useful in getting your homepage up to the standard you're happy with.

5. The Checklist:
*1. Logo Placement:*
The first thing your users should see is your logo Heat map testing tools consistently indicate top-left is a common hot spot on the page, which is why will mostly see logos there.
Make sure you are using your logo or a part of your logo as your favicon. This is the icon you’ll see on the tab of your web browser. Make sure it's easily distinguishable. As you can see here the favicon of Rockdale Music & Studios is the most easily recognizable. Although this is partly due to the design of their full logo.

![image581.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image581.png)

*Make sure you are using your logo or a part of your logo as your favicon. This is the icon you’ll see on the tab of your web browser. Make sure it's easily distinguishable. *
* As you can see here the favicon of Rockdale Music & Studios is the most easily recognizable. Although this is partly due to the design of their full logo.*

![image579.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image579.png)

*2. Heading:*
Your main heading/headline should be attention-grabbing and to the point.
It also pays to think about click-through continuity. Your homepage headline should offer continuity to the medium and message that lead visitors there. So if your advertising outside your site features a key idea with keywords, restate them in your headline.

*3. Subheading:*
Elaborate on what has been said by the heading and avoid jargon.

![image576.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image576.png)
*These are all good headlines and sub-headlines. It's worth noting that you want to make sure your image is dark or light enough for the text that's over it. Or alternatively, you can use text boxes like Fermata Music School Lucan has.*

*4. Call to Action (CTA):*
Your primary CTA should be as few words as possible. Ideally, you shouldn't have more than 2-3 calls to action in your first section.
Most can’t-miss calls to action are often in the centre and are tied in with the hero image. A hero image is the main image in the first section of your page
It can be effective to start with action words so that it reads like a command. Make the first word of your CTA a verb (that isn’t “click”).
It can be useful but not essential to highlight the value. Your CTA could explicitly explain the value of acting, such as “Get your free trial”.

![image573.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image573.png)

*5. Branding:*
Broadly speaking this is any consistent element across all interactions people have with your business.
In this case, it's the consistency of colour scheme, font, logo, imagery, copywriting style etc. You want your site to have a distinctive look and feel.
This is about communicating the visual identity of your business. It leaves a strong impression and makes it easy for your online and offline content to feel unified and purposeful.

![image562.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image562.png)

*6. Imagery:*
Avoid stock photos.
Your homepage is an opportunity to show people what the experiences your school has to offer actually look like.
Your images should have a purpose. You can even use them to qualify your audience. This typically means photos of smiling students playing instruments.

![image559.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image559.png)

*7. Social Media Links:*
You should link to your main social media pages somewhere on your home page. This is usually done on the header, the footer or both. Use icons. They should be small and not in the centre because they are important but not a high priority.

![image556.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image556.png)

*8. Hero Section:*
This is just the first section of your website. Although in this case what we recommend applies to the above the fold section which is everything visible on your website when it first loads which might include part of your second section as well.
You should have a strong hero image and a clear Call to Action.
 You don’t want the focal point of your home page to be busy. Make it purposeful and easy to understand.

![image554.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image554.png)
![image567.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image567.png)

*9. Main Menu/Navigation Bar:*
Have as few menu items as you possibly can.
Avoid hamburger icons (except on mobile) because they reduce discoverability.

![image544.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image544.png)

*10. Lead Magnet Section:*
Lead magnets are offers of free content in exchange for a visitor's name and email. Having a section for this isn't essential (and I'm yet to find a school that does this on their homepage), it's just one way to generate new leads.

*11. Features/Benefits Section:*
There are many ways to do this as you’ll see. Typically this means a grid of short paragraphs each with a title and an icon. Icons make people understand the feature or benefit faster and don't slow a page down as much as images.
This section can be a good opportunity to communicate your school's point of difference.

![image543.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image543.png)

*12. About Section:*
A brief section about who you are is useful to have early on your homepage potentially with a button that links to an about page.

![image552.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image552.png)

#### About Section Copy Example
About us section should be written with the customer in mind. It’s not a brag book.
The about section on our music school section went something like this:

*"Hi there. *
*My name is Jonny Wilson, founder of Goodtime Music Academy. When I was a young drummer, I had an incredible drum teacher called Chris. Chris wasn’t just my teacher, he quickly became a mentor, an encourager, and someone I aspired to be like when I grew up. *
*In fact he inspired me so much that I went on to complete a music degree from the NZ school of music. From there I then built Goodtime Music Academy so that as many people as possible could experience what it’s like to learn in an environment where it’s not just about the music.*
*I believe that learning positive hobbies with positive role models is one of the most beneficial things a young person can do to improve their future. *
*Being devoted to our students success in both music and in life has led us to grow from 30 students learning in an old shed to 2000+ students and a team of 60 people. We’ve also been lucky enough to win a bunch of prestigious awards (see pictures below). *
*My story would be radically different if my parents didn’t drive me to all those music lessons… even on the weeks I hadn’t practiced and they may have questioned the cost. ;p *
*If you see me around the academy, make sure to say hi. :)"*

*13. Social Proof/Testimonials:*
If you have them there should be a section for them on your home page.
Sliders are ideal for these sections because they don’t take up much room.
Full images or even just faces in small circles work well here.

![image550.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image550.png)

*14. Blog or News Section:*
 A section with recent blog posts or news updates. You don’t need more than three posts or updates here since the type of section is just a sample that links to a blog or news page.
This is good for increasing engagement in new users as news and events give the impression of activity and success. You want to show people how eventful your school is.
Blog pages are also a good opportunity for educational content or customer communication. There will be more on the topic of blog pages later in the module.

![image548.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image548.png)

*15. Success Indicators:*
This includes any awards, achievements or statistics on your business. Anything that positions you as an authority/success. This section doesn’t need to take up much room.

![image546.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image546.png)

*16. Footer with Quick Links:*
This makes your site easier to navigate.
 It should include all the links in your main menu as well as your social media links, contact and location information, as well links to any policy pages you have.
 It can also include additional links that you didn’t include on your main menu in order not to overload it.
![image540.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image540.png)

*17. Subscribe/Contact Form:*
This is an opportunity for a lead or enquiry without users having to leave the page. It works well near the bottom of your page and it can even be a part of your footer.

![image538.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image538.png)

*18. Back to the Top Button:*
 Another feature that makes the site easier to navigate and takes up almost no space.

![image536.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image536.png)

*19. Special Entry Points:*
Login buttons or student/teacher/parent portals. These are best placed early in the page or in the main menu without being distracting.

![image534.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image534.png)

# Home Page Promo Videos
*Coming Up In This Course*
This course is mainly looking at promo videos to introduce and promote your whole school, not an individual programme. However we do give some examples of these so you can see the differences, and get ideas for those if you need, as a lot of the skills and tips are transferable. What you will find in this course:
9 reasons why you should have a promo video
Tips for creating a promo video yourself
Some amazing #BAMSQUAD promo videos examples
#BAMSQUAD individual programme promo videos
#BAMSQUAD promo videos in other languages

*9 Reasons Why You Should Have a Promo Video*
Videos create a human connection between businesses and customers.
They don’t need your full attention and content can be picked up passively, which is why videos are so popular. They are less time-consuming than reading information, and less demanding on the viewer. Many customers actually prefer watching videos.
Brings your school to life with footage, movement, music and you talking directly to them… Reading off a page isn’t anywhere near as powerful.
Shows your audience what they’ll experience if they attend and they can see others enjoying the experience.
If people can visualise a product in action it makes them believe in it and want it.
People get to meet you (via video) and decide if they like you. This might make you nervous, but consider how much easier it is for a parent to be able to introduce their child to you before they turn up for their first lesson. Someone who doesn’t have a video leaves students/parents going into the unknown. This is a great way to build trust.
Promotional videos can be shared over a wide spectrum of digital platforms.
Once you have your promo video you can use it multiple times without needing to repeatedly spend funds on disposable physical materials.
People from all walks of life can engage in film and videos.

*Tips for Creating a Promo Video Yourself *
Storyboard your vision and script it out - If you find a video clip you like you can transcribe it, then tweak it to make it your own. There are online transcribing tools that will save you time. For example:
Write down the types of B-roll shots you’d like to collect. Make a list to capture anything missing that you don’t have, or can’t get from the BAM library. Here are some examples of B-roll footage you could capture:
Students engaging with the teacher
Students playing over different instruments
Teacher and student high fiving
Group performances
Other things you have going on in your school, these might not be music related such as holiday programmes
Footage of the teaching rooms
Footage of the equipment
Students making friends
Students smiling/ laughing
Examples of your videos or books, if you have created your own
Checkout our example videos from the #BAMSQUAD below so get more ideas!
You want to get the following qualities in your video:
Communication - either through text or A-roll footage
Storytelling - give your prospective customers a brief outline of your why, what they will gain from coming to your school? What happens there?
Engagement - Mix your storytelling with engaging B-roll footage showing off your school so they can see what’s happening and what it looks like.
Human connection - Show B-roll footage of students having fun, being engaged, making friends, high fiving their teacher etc.
Select your music at the start and edit to the music - choose a piece that’s not too long and be aware of any copyright restrictions. It’s easier to edit a video to the music than to cut the video and have to try and find music that finishes at the exact time you want.
Reflect back to your brand and your ‘why’. What are the values and the personality of your music school that you want to shine through in your video. When watching back your video, ask yourself if it ticks those boxes.
Ensure your video is quick and attention grabbing, not too much in-depth detail. Use fast cuts in your edit, as well as catchy sound bites or titles.
Visual titles to help the viewers know what to remember, make use of them for your key points.
Integrate your brand colours and logos throughout your video. It will have a lasting impact on the audience and make your brand more memorable.
Make sure you can have subtitles on your video to make it more accessible if needed.
Don’t make it too long! Ensure the most important information is in the first 15-30 seconds. As much as you’ll love the whole video, most people won’t watch the whole thing... You don’t want them to miss the call to action.
Make the ‘call to action’ very clear in your video. What should they do to sign up? How should they contact you? Use text here to make it extremely clear too.
Make your video timeless by not adding information such as lesson prices, meaning you will be able to use your video for longer before needing to update it.
Promote your promo video by loading it into YouTube, which acts as a search engine, therefore it will rank higher in google - Label it something people would search e.g. Music Lessons [your town/city name]
On Youtube ensure you hyperlink your website in the description for easy accessibility.
A lot of views come from mobiles phones so make sure your video (and website) looks good on a mobile device - make sure you watch it through on mobile before you release it. See if there’s any red flags that jump out.
Think about creating a few versions of your video to share on multiple platforms. E.G. people have shorter attention spans on instagram compared to YouTube.

*#BAMSQUAD Promo Video Examples: *
Check out some epic #BAMSQUAD home page promo videos below. We have also listed somethings that we think makes it great, to watch out for, so you can think about including that in your video!
Jonny's Promo Video from Good Time Music Academy - Great use of B-roll showing a variety of different type of footage such as performances, lessons, teacher/student relationship, effective use of text to highlight key points, contact details at the end.

Jonny's FIRST Promo Video from Good Time Music Academy.

Tim Woosley's Promo Video for Lincoln School of Music - Good connection with a teacher, looking at positives of the lessons other than learning an instruments, great use of B roll footage.

Monster Music Promo Video - Great examples of A and B roll, great timing of the video, cool shots like student and teacher high-fiving.

Inspire Music Promo Video - Good way to meet the teacher and connect with prospective customers.

Glasgow Guitar Academy - Cool and creative camera shots, students having a great time and connection with their teacher, effective music.

Fort Worth Music Academy - Fast paced and short video with the essential information.

iRock Music School - Nice introduction footage used, good use of text with A & B roll, lesson footage and performance footage to show off the school.

Let There Be Rock Schools - Cool voice over making it like a trailer, use of text to capture key points, B roll footage and it captures the feel of the school well.

Music Tech Studios - This video has a great use of B roll footage showing off the school and what's on offer paired with text to give more information.

Limitless Music Academy - Great use of 'feel good music' to break up the talking sections, able to connect with a teacher in the video, awesome use of lesson/ performing footage to show off the instruments and programme.

DK Drums - Very professional production with lots of information, interaction, text and footage. DK drums offers an online course with a different type of programme, therefore it is a longer promo video.

Dave from Piano Jam - Great use of pictures and information in the video along with subtitles.

# Main Offer Pages
Sometimes a music school will offer so many programs, it can be hard to choose the best way to showcase this to potential families. This lesson will guide you through breaking down your offers into what's most important to showcase first, to new leads.
You want to make sure that no matter how many things your music school offers, such as holiday programmes, in-person guitar classes or even online glee classes... that everything is grouped in an easy-to-navigate way.
Use this lesson for inspiration to break your offers up into easy categories for users to understand.
*Contents:*
Purpose - main offer/service page overview
 How a dedicated services page benefits your website
Main offer page - various elements that are important to service pages with examples
Checklist - An explanation of the list item followed by examples and comments
Full Examples - ten main offer page examples

1. Purpose
Your main offer is your most prominent offer. Typically for a medium to large-sized music school, this means a music lessons page that breaks down lesson types; group, private, in school, online, and from there it links to a page for that subcategory offering different instruments.

This is effectively your services page. It breaks down your main services into categories and subcategories. Smaller music schools with fewer offers might be able to do this effectively on a home page. But for most other music schools, the main offers page is good for showing visitors the full scope of your services and it helps send them down the right path by laying out the benefits of each category of offers on the same page so they can be more easily compared.

This is especially useful if you have a page for each category of services. Because it provides that comparison overview for customers who are interested in what you have to offer and don't have specific kinds of lessons in mind.

2. How a Dedicated Services Page Benefits Your Website:
Sometimes, people will end up on your website because they’re looking for a very specific type of solution. However, a lot of visitors aren’t likely to know who you are and what you do until they arrive. Communicating your business purpose and offerings to those potential leads should be one of your site’s primary goals.

3. Main offer page:
These are the main elements you should consider including on your main offer page.

*Hero Section:*
Describe your service and its benefits in the headline. The job of your page’s headline or title is to attract and hold your customer’s attention long enough to keep them reading your page. So if the title doesn’t immediately demonstrate how you solve their problem, they’ll have little to no reason to stay. It's also important to use a hero image as the first section on this page in combination with the headline.

![image386.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image386.png)
![image402.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image402.png)

![image399.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image399.png)

*Features/Benefits:*
This is a secondary place on your website (the primary place being your homepage), to highlight the benefits of becoming a student of your music school. You can also highlight the benefits or features of each lesson type in the short paragraphs of text you typically see near the call to action for an offer.
![image396.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image396.png)

![image393.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image393.png)
 
*FAQ Section:*
This saves time by putting answers in one place. It preempts questions that would otherwise go into an initial enquiry since most contact pages on music school websites have enquiry sections. This isn't a section you see often on music school websites, but as long as you've had enough time to have frequently asked questions it's a good idea to have it on your website, especially since it doesn't take up much space.
![image382.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image382.png)
 
*Testimonials:*
Testimonial sections are definitely a type of section you can use on multiple pages on your website, especially on pages with a features or benefits section because they reinforce and back up your claims. They can be especially useful if you have testimonials that are specific to the content on the page it is on or the sections next to it. This doesn't need to be extensive, if you have a decent collection that's great, but all you really need is one highly relevant quote from a customer as you'll see in two of the three examples.
![image379.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image379.png)
![image372.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image372.png)
![image470.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image470.png)

*Pricing:*
This isn't something essential for all music schools. If you decide you want to have a pricing section this is the place for it. Placing this information on this page also means it won’t clutter up your homepage. Trying to cram a lot of details about your services on your homepage can be counterproductive. In general, you want your services page to be concise and easy to grasp. Your pricing doesn't necessarily need its own section, it could just be included in the lesson types section.

![image469.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image469.png)
![image467.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image467.png)
![image465.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image465.png)

# About / Team Pages
Often left till last, the about page is an integral part of your website. It's the point of difference that helps your music school stand out, and connect to your website visitors in a special and unique way. This lesson will help you design an about page that gets people lining up to visit your music school specifically!
Important things to remember is that your story and your face as the school founder need to be on this page! You also want to keep it about how you can help the customer and not just about how great your school is without relating it back to your end-user - the student and their family!
*Contents:*
The #1 thing people get wrong with About Pages
Purpose  - about page overview and function
Common Issues
Team/about page crossover
Key sections that your about page should include
Conclusion
Full Examples - All of the about pages used here in full

1.The #1 thing people get wrong with about pages:
Your About us section should be written with the customer in mind. It’s not a brag book.
Tell your story in a way that you tell the customer what's in it for them if they learn with you.
Don't list every single musical achievement you've had, most people don't read it, nor do they care.
My about section is a great example of how to tell your story about your music school while sprinkling in some achievements without it turning into a brag fest.
This is a great model to follow because it can also double as your elevator pitch when  you explain to people what you do for a job.

*Hi there. *
*My name is Jonny Wilson, founder of Goodtime Music Academy.*
*When I was a young drummer, I had an incredible drum teacher called Chris.*
*Chris wasn’t just my teacher, he quickly became a mentor, an encourager, and someone I aspired to be like when I grew up. *
*In fact he inspired me so much that I went on to complete a music degree from the NZ school of music. From there I then built Goodtime Music Academy so that as many people as possible could experience what it’s like to learn in an environment where it’s not just about the music.*
*I believe that learning positive hobbies with positive role models is one of the most beneficial things a young person can do to improve their future. *
*Being devoted to our students success in both music and in life has led us to grow from 30 students learning in an old shed to 2000+ students and a team of 60 people. We’ve also been lucky enough to win a bunch of prestigious awards (see pictures below). *
*My story would be radically different if my parents didn’t drive me to all those music lessons… even on the weeks I hadn’t practiced and they may have questioned the cost. ;)*
*If you see me around the academy, make sure to say hi. :) *

2. Purpose:
There are many names for this type of page: about, our story, our mission, meet the team etc. They all have the same purpose. When a visitor wants to learn more about you or your business and about the people behind the business, the about page is the page they’ll look for. Why should they get involved with your music school?

3. Common Issues:
Most about us pages are an afterthought, a missed opportunity or worse a dead end. About pages should put a face to your music school, featuring the founders or the people on your team.

4. Team/About Page Crossover:
Often these two page types are combined. It's up to you to decide whether it's worth separating or just linking between the two. The deciding factor might be whether you have enough content for a substantial team page. What is important is there are no dead ends. You'll see in the examples how none of them just end with text sections. There is always a call to action.

5. Key Sections That Your About Page Should Include:

*Our Story:*
How your school came about (often this is just the first text section on an about page).

![image781.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image781.png)

![image699.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image699.png)
![image697.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image697.png)
*This specific example (above), is an about section on the home page of this music school's website. *

*The People:*
Your team, your teachers, you. It's important to introduce yourself as the owner of the school.

![image695.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image695.png)

![image693.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image693.png)

![image707.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image707.png)

*Your Mission + Values:*
People want to figure out if they share core values with your school.

![image705.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image705.png)
![image703.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image703.png)
![image701.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image701.png)

Video:
This could be a promo video or a virtual tour of your school, or an our story video that's more about your mission and values than your services.
![image692.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image692.png)
![image691.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image691.png)

*6. Conclusion:*
Overall what matters is that you take advantage of the about page. Expand on benefits and features of your school that tie in with your story. This is an opportunity to use visual that you didn't have room for on your home page.

# Policy Pages
The main purpose of this lesson is to give you an overview and examples of the main types of policy pages music schools have on their websites. We won't go into great detail on any particular type of policy page except for privacy policy pages (because almost all music schools will need this in some form).
As a general rule, if you're collecting user information on a website like a person's name and contact details, your website will need a privacy policy. This doesn't need to be intimidating, however. This lesson will help you create a privacy policy quickly and in a visually appealing way. We've also included some places you can access free online support to get your own policy made, quickly and easily!

*Contents:*
Policy page types - overview and examples
Placement - where you link to them on your site
Design
Examples

*1. Policy page types:*
Having your policy pages on your website is an easy way to protect your business from complaints. Most of these pages aren't going to be legally required, they are just another layer of clear communication with your customers and the people who visit your website.
Certain types of pages may be specific to your music school and will be time-consuming to create, while others can be made quickly using templates. You will always be able to free examples of generic policies like terms and conditions policies for using a website, that you can copy and edit to suit your needs.
You can generate your own privacy using one of these free online tools below, or check out our lesson in module 3 under 'S = Systems' called 'Creating and Implementing Your T&C's/ Policies' which has loads of examples and a template to work from.

* *
These are the types of policy pages music schools generally have on their websites (this does not mean you need to have every one of these):
Privacy policy
Terms & conditions
Lesson policies
Health and safety policies
Child protection policy
COVID-19 protection policy
Whether you are legally required to have certain policies on your website will also depend on your country. As a general rule if you are collecting personal information you will need a privacy policy or a terms and conditions policy that covers what you will and won't do with personal information. If you are collecting names in a contact form or running online ads, you need a privacy policy.

*2. Placement:*
Typically your policy pages will go in the footer of every page on your website. That way it's always there but it's not distracting. Some websites might have pages explaining lesson policies or COVID-19 policies in more prominent places. Because of the value of being as upfront with this information as possible. You may also want to include a link to your privacy policy on any forms where you are directly collecting information. The most common and convenient place for this is in your footer or in a narrow section just below it.
![image753.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image753.png)
![image751.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image751.png)

*3. Design:*
Fortunately, these pages don't need any time-consuming design considerations. They are going to be the simplest pages on your websites as you'll see below in most of the examples from other music schools. That being said, if you have multiple policy pages that you want people to be able to access in an obvious location you might consider an information page that links to all your policy pages, a drop down menu where all the pages can be accessed, or just a list of links on your footer under a heading.

# Blog/News Pages
A regularly updated blog page is great for both your SEO and the way your school stands out to potential customers. This lesson will address best practices for maintaining a current blog or news page, and how to lay it out in an easy-to-navigate way.
Remember, there's a difference between blog and news pages. Blogs are articles about anything music-related at all! However, news pages serve to regularly update your potential families on the happenings in your school. Consider if a blog or news page is right for you as you process through this lesson.

*Contents:*
Blog pages vs. news pages - overview and differences
Blog search features + category sections
Author name and photo + published dates section
Share buttons and comment sections
Email signup section and calls to action
Readability
Using landing page elements
Full examples (of both the blog/news pages and post examples)
**Disclaimer: We never used typical blog pages in growing our school. It was something we dabbled with early on, but realised that for us, it wasn't worth the time, nor was there enough traffic to justify the investment. That doesn't mean it's not worth pursuing, but let me tell you what works better than blogs like 'What age should your child start piano lessons?'. *
*Blogs like 'Top 10 kids activities in [your city]. Think of things parents would love to click on. Then rank your music school in the list with links to a compelling offer or for a 2-4-1 voucher etc. We talk about this concept in more depth in Module 5. *

*1. Blog Pages vs. News Pages:*
A blog serves as a space to discuss key topics related to music lessons and school in general, not just your own. Having blog posts that relate to music education as a whole is a great way to build yourself up as an authority in your industry. Doing this also improves your SEO (search engine optimization), because using search terms your potential customers use in your blog posts makes your website more discoverable.
The News section of your site should be a timeline of your music school's updates and announcements. This is where you announce new hires, upcoming events, or changes to your lessons or other offers. You can think of your news section as another platform to post on, which can list lots of the informative posts you create on your Facebook page already!
Ideally, your blog and your news/updates section should be separated because they do different things. There will be people who don't care about your blog but might be interested in the updates that a news section has.
Just having a blog or a news section does not make it worthwhile, and not keeping them current can actually send a negative message. Regularly posting blogs or news sends the message that your school is eventful and constantly improving and updating itself.
The rest of this post is going to focus on features of blog pages and posts that you should consider when making or improving your blog.

*2. Blog Search Features + Category Sections:*
If your blog is a resource with a long list of posts on a variety of topics it might make it easier to navigate if there is a search tool and a list of categories visitors can use to narrow their search.

![image1000.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image1000.png)

![image989.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image989.png)
![image986.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image986.png)

* *
*3. Author Name and Photo + Published Dates Section:*
Seeing the author’s face (or at least their name) helps build a connection between the reader and the content. It makes the content more engaging and human-centred.
Most blogs show the date the post was published. Some show two, the date published and the date updated. If you are updating your blog regularly you should highlight this fact.
![image983.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image983.png)
![image980.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image980.png)
![image996.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image996.png)

*4. Share Buttons and Comment Sections:*
This is a common feature on blogs. There are a lot of places you can add share buttons. So where do share buttons appear in blog posts? Often in more than one place.
Comment sections often follow share buttons. They aren't an essential feature but they are still worth considering.

![image994.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image994.png)
![image960.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image960.png)
![image971.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image971.png)
![image969.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image969.png)

*5. Email Signup Section and Calls to Action:*
*
*People who start their journey on your site on a blog post might not be likely to become a lead, but that doesn't mean they are unlikely to subscribe. One of the best outcomes of having a blog reader is a new subscriber.
![image966.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image966.png)
![image963.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image963.png)
![image955.png](2. Business Backbones_images/image955.png)

*6. Readability:*
Your blog should be as easy to read as possible. Especially if your blog posts are long, what often matters most in the design of your blog is readability. People come to your blog to read. And if that text content is hard to read, the blog will have low engagement. Not only should your blog be easy to read, it should be easy to skim, since that's often how people will engage with it.
This means using wide margins and creating bold sub-headers that break long posts into small sections. For long blog posts use a linked table of contents and give a quick summary of the main points at the start of the post.

*7. Using Landing Page Elements:*
Lastly, it's important to take the lead from landing pages and the way they are structured. Landing pages are made to generate leads. You might build a landing page to encourage users to download an information pack, sign up for a free trial, or enroll a student. The main goal of a landing page is to encourage the reader to take an action (and as in many other parts of this module on music school websites we encourage you to use elements of landing pages across your website).
